From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia


Marvel comic book series
For the eponymous team and various media properties, see [X\-Men](/wiki/X-Men "X-Men"). For the video game, see [The Uncanny X\-Men (video game)](/wiki/The_Uncanny_X-Men_(video_game) "The Uncanny X-Men (video game)"). For the Australian band, see [Uncanny X\-Men (band)](/wiki/Uncanny_X-Men_(band) "Uncanny X-Men (band)").
For [technical reasons](/wiki/Wikipedia:Naming_conventions_(technical_restrictions) "Wikipedia:Naming conventions (technical restrictions)"), "Uncanny X\-Men \#94" redirects here. For the series' issue \#94, see [Uncanny X\-Men 94](/wiki/Uncanny_X-Men_94 "Uncanny X-Men 94").

***Uncanny X\-Men***, originally published as ***The X\-Men***, is an [American comic book](/wiki/American_comic_book "American comic book") series published by [Marvel Comics](/wiki/Marvel_Comics "Marvel Comics") since 1963, and is the longest\-running series in the [X\-Men comics franchise](/wiki/List_of_X-Men_comics "List of X-Men comics"). It features a team of [superheroes](/wiki/Superhero "Superhero") called the [X\-Men](/wiki/X-Men "X-Men"), a group of [mutants](/wiki/Mutant_(Marvel_Comics) "Mutant (Marvel Comics)") with superhuman abilities led and taught by [Professor X](/wiki/Professor_X "Professor X").


The title was created by [Stan Lee](/wiki/Stan_Lee "Stan Lee") and [Jack Kirby](/wiki/Jack_Kirby "Jack Kirby"), met with a lukewarm reception, and eventually became a reprints\-only book in 1970\. Interest was rekindled with 1975's *[Giant\-Size X\-Men](/wiki/Giant-Size_X-Men "Giant-Size X-Men")* \#1 and the debut of a new, international team. Initially under the guidance of artist [David Cockrum](/wiki/David_Cockrum "David Cockrum"), writer Len Wein, and especially writer [Chris Claremont](/wiki/Chris_Claremont "Chris Claremont") whose 16\-year stint began with August 1975's [*Uncanny X\-Men* \#94](/wiki/Uncanny_X-Men_94 "Uncanny X-Men 94"), the series grew in popularity worldwide, eventually spawning a franchise with numerous spin\-off "X\-books" including *[New Mutants](/wiki/The_New_Mutants_(comic_book) "The New Mutants (comic book)")*, *[X\-Factor](/wiki/X-Factor_(comic_book) "X-Factor (comic book)")*, *[Excalibur](/wiki/Excalibur_(comics) "Excalibur (comics)")*, *[X\-Force](/wiki/X-Force_(comic_book) "X-Force (comic book)")*, *[Generation X](/wiki/Generation_X_(comic_book) "Generation X (comic book)")*, and other flagship titles like the simply titled *[X\-Men](/wiki/X-Men:_Legacy "X-Men: Legacy")* (later *[New X\-Men](/wiki/New_X-Men_(2001_series) "New X-Men (2001 series)")* and *[X\-Men Legacy](/wiki/X-Men_Legacy "X-Men Legacy")*), *[Astonishing X\-Men](/wiki/Astonishing_X-Men "Astonishing X-Men")*, *[All\-New X\-Men](/wiki/All-New_X-Men "All-New X-Men")*, *[Amazing X\-Men](/wiki/Amazing_X-Men "Amazing X-Men")*, *[Extraordinary X\-Men](/wiki/Extraordinary_X-Men "Extraordinary X-Men")* and *[X\-Men Gold](/wiki/X-Men_Gold "X-Men Gold")*.




Publication history
-------------------

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: Publication history")]
### 1963–1970: Original run

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: 1963–1970: Original run")]
Created by writer\-editor [Stan Lee](/wiki/Stan_Lee "Stan Lee") and artist [Jack Kirby](/wiki/Jack_Kirby "Jack Kirby"), the series launched in September 1963,[\[1]](#cite_note-1)[\[2]](#cite_note-2) introducing in its first issue the original five X\-Men ([Warren Worthington III/Angel](/wiki/Warren_Worthington_III "Warren Worthington III"), [Hank McCoy/Beast](/wiki/Beast_(comics) "Beast (comics)"), [Scott Summers/Cyclops](/wiki/Cyclops_(Marvel_Comics) "Cyclops (Marvel Comics)"), [Robert "Bobby" Drake/Iceman](/wiki/Iceman_(comics) "Iceman (comics)"), and [Jean Grey/Marvel Girl](/wiki/Jean_Grey "Jean Grey")) and their teacher, [Charles Xavier/Professor X](/wiki/Professor_X "Professor X") as well as their nemesis, the [supervillain](/wiki/Supervillain "Supervillain") [Erik Magnus Lehnsherr/Magneto](/wiki/Magneto_(Marvel_Comics) "Magneto (Marvel Comics)").[\[3]](#cite_note-3) Although Lee would deny it, it was noticed by contemporary writer [Arnold Drake](/wiki/Arnold_Drake "Arnold Drake"), that the concept of the series emulated his own earlier series for [National Periodical Publications](/wiki/National_Periodical_Publications "National Periodical Publications")'s (now [DC Comics](/wiki/DC_Comics "DC Comics")) *[The Doom Patrol](/wiki/The_Doom_Patrol "The Doom Patrol")*, in many respects. However, National's editorial staff did not support Drake's concerns.[\[4]](#cite_note-4)


Initially published bimonthly, it became a monthly with issue \#14 (November 1965\). Lee's run lasted 19 issues, and featured the X\-Men battling villains such as Magneto's [Brotherhood of Mutants](/wiki/Brotherhood_of_Mutants "Brotherhood of Mutants") (which included the siblings [Wanda Maximoff/the Scarlet Witch](/wiki/Scarlet_Witch "Scarlet Witch") and [Pietro Maximoff/Quicksilver](/wiki/Quicksilver_(comics) "Quicksilver (comics)"));[\[5]](#cite_note-5) the [Sentinels](/wiki/Sentinels_(comics) "Sentinels (comics)"), giant robots programmed to destroy all mutants, and their creator [Bolivar Trask](/wiki/Bolivar_Trask "Bolivar Trask"); and [Cain Marko/the Juggernaut](/wiki/Juggernaut_(comics) "Juggernaut (comics)"), Xavier's stepbrother transformed by a mystical gem and seeking revenge on Xavier.


The series was placed firmly in the [Marvel Universe](/wiki/Marvel_Universe "Marvel Universe"), with guest appearances by [Namor MacKenzie/Namor the Sub\-Mariner](/wiki/Namor_(comics) "Namor (comics)") in \#6 and the [Avengers](/wiki/Avengers_(comics) "Avengers (comics)") in \#9\. The jungle adventure hero [Kevin Plunder/Ka\-Zar](/wiki/Ka-Zar_(Kevin_Plunder) "Ka-Zar (Kevin Plunder)") and the [Savage Land](/wiki/Savage_Land "Savage Land") were introduced in issue \#10\.[\[6]](#cite_note-6) [Roy Thomas](/wiki/Roy_Thomas "Roy Thomas") wrote the series from \#20–43 (May 1966 – April 1968\). Thomas and artist [Werner Roth](/wiki/Werner_Roth_(comics) "Werner Roth (comics)") created [Sean Cassidy/the Banshee](/wiki/Banshee_(comics) "Banshee (comics)") in \#28 (Jan. 1967\).[\[7]](#cite_note-7)[\[8]](#cite_note-defalco-8) *The X\-Men* \#45 (June 1968\) featured a crossover with *The [Avengers](/wiki/Avengers_(comics) "Avengers (comics)")* \#53 (June 1968\).[\[9]](#cite_note-9)[\[10]](#cite_note-10) After brief runs by [Gary Friedrich](/wiki/Gary_Friedrich "Gary Friedrich") and [Arnold Drake](/wiki/Arnold_Drake "Arnold Drake") – the latter of whom introduced the new X\-Men [Lorna Dane/Polaris](/wiki/Polaris_(Marvel_Comics) "Polaris (Marvel Comics)")[\[11]](#cite_note-11) and [Alex Summers/Havok](/wiki/Havok_(comics) "Havok (comics)"),[\[12]](#cite_note-12) and during which the series adopted a new logo designed by [Jim Steranko](/wiki/Jim_Steranko "Jim Steranko") – Thomas returned to the series with issue \#55 and was joined by artist [Neal Adams](/wiki/Neal_Adams "Neal Adams") the following issue for an acclaimed run of stories.[\[13]](#cite_note-13)[\[14]](#cite_note-14) After a battle with the [Hulk](/wiki/Hulk "Hulk") in issue \#66 (March 1970\),[\[15]](#cite_note-15) the title ceased publishing original material and featured reprints in issues \#67–93 (December 1970 – April 1975\).



### 1970–1975: Other appearances

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: 1970–1975: Other appearances")]
Despite the title going into reprints for the next five years between \#67–93, the X\-Men continued to appear in other Marvel titles throughout this period in a guest\-starring capacity or cameo appearance, either as a team or in solo adventures.[\[16]](#cite_note-16)


The Angel appeared without the X\-Men in a three\-part story involving the murder of his father at the hands of his uncle, Burt Worthington, a.k.a. the Dazzler, in *Ka\-Zar* \#2 (Dec. 1970\) and \#3 (March 1971\), and *[Marvel Tales](/wiki/Marvel_Tales_(comics) "Marvel Tales (comics)")* \#30 (April 1971\).[\[17]](#cite_note-17)


Iceman made a guest appearance in *[Amazing Spider\-Man](/wiki/Amazing_Spider-Man "Amazing Spider-Man")* \#92 (Jan. 1971\), where he battles Spider\-Man after mistakenly assuming he abducted Gwen Stacy. Later, after realizing his error, they team up against corrupt politician, Sam Bullitt.[\[18]](#cite_note-18) Iceman appears alone once again in *[Marvel Team\-Up](/wiki/Marvel_Team-Up "Marvel Team-Up")* \#23 (July 1974\), where he teams up with the [Human Torch](/wiki/Human_Torch "Human Torch") to battle the [Equinox](/wiki/Equinox_(comics) "Equinox (comics)"). Cyclops, Marvel Girl, and Angel also make a brief appearance.[\[19]](#cite_note-19)


The Beast starred in his own series in *[Amazing Adventures](/wiki/Amazing_Adventures "Amazing Adventures")* \#11–17 (March 1972 – March 1973\) after accepting a position with the [Brand Corporation](/wiki/Roxxon_Energy_Corporation "Roxxon Energy Corporation"), and decides to leave the X\-Men for good. The original team appeared briefly in a flashback in \#11 (March 1972\) and in a present\-day cameo appearance in \#15 (November 1972\). Professor Xavier and Marvel Girl briefly appeared in *Amazing Adventures* \#12 (May 1972\), and the Angel helped the Beast defeat the [Griffin](/wiki/Griffin_(Marvel_Comics) "Griffin (Marvel Comics)") in *Amazing Adventures* \#15\.[\[20]](#cite_note-20) After his run on that series ended, the Beast battled against the Hulk alone in *Incredible Hulk* \#161 (March 1973\), concluding a storyline which began in *Amazing Adventures*.[\[21]](#cite_note-21)


Havok and Polaris, as recounted in a flashback in *[Incredible Hulk](/wiki/Incredible_Hulk_(comic_book) "Incredible Hulk (comic book)")* \#150 (April 1972\), leave the X\-Men after a bitter quarrel with Iceman and move to New Mexico. In a present\-day appearance in the same issue, they encounter and battle the Hulk, who mistakes Polaris for his former lover [Jarella](/wiki/Jarella "Jarella"), and a biker gang.[\[22]](#cite_note-22)


As a team, the X\-Men assisted Spider\-Man in capturing [Michael Morbius](/wiki/Michael_Morbius "Michael Morbius") in *[Marvel Team\-Up](/wiki/Marvel_Team-Up "Marvel Team-Up")* \#4 (Sept. 1972\), written by [Gerry Conway](/wiki/Gerry_Conway "Gerry Conway"), after one of Xavier's colleagues is abducted by the vampire.[\[23]](#cite_note-23) In *[Adventure into Fear](/wiki/Adventure_into_Fear "Adventure into Fear")* \#20 (Feb. 1974\), it is recounted in a flashback how Morbius escaped from the X\-Men's mansion. Professor Xavier and Cyclops appeared in a brief cameo.[\[24]](#cite_note-24)


They appeared in *[The Avengers](/wiki/The_Avengers_(comic_book) "The Avengers (comic book)")* \#110–111 (April–May 1973\), written by [Steve Englehart](/wiki/Steve_Englehart "Steve Englehart"), where Professor Xavier and the X\-Men are abducted by Magneto after an ambush at Xavier's mansion, and later rescued by the Avengers, Daredevil, and the Black Widow.[\[25]](#cite_note-25) Soon after, members of the X\-Men, including Iceman, Angel, Beast, Havok and Polaris, and members of the Brotherhood of Evil Mutants are secretly being abducted by a group of hooded figures known as the [Secret Empire](/wiki/Secret_Empire_(organization) "Secret Empire (organization)"). At this point, only Professor Xavier, Cyclops and Marvel Girl remain active, and, after a brief cameo at the end of *Incredible Hulk* \#172 (Feb. 1974\), where they discover an unconscious Juggernaut after a battle with the Hulk,[\[26]](#cite_note-26) seek out the help of Captain America and the Falcon. This led to the first "Secret Empire" storyline, which ran in *[Captain America](/wiki/Captain_America_(comic_book) "Captain America (comic book)")* \#172–175 (April–July 1974\), also written by Steve Englehart.[\[27]](#cite_note-27)


Professor Xavier made a few brief appearances of his own without the X\-Men during this time. In *The Avengers* \#88 (May 1971\), written by [Roy Thomas](/wiki/Roy_Thomas "Roy Thomas") and [Harlan Ellison](/wiki/Harlan_Ellison "Harlan Ellison"), he made a cameo appearance assisting Reed Richards and [General Thunderbolt Ross](/wiki/General_Thunderbolt_Ross "General Thunderbolt Ross") in containing the Hulk,[\[28]](#cite_note-28) and later appeared briefly onscreen in *Shanna the She\-Devil* \#5 (Aug. 1973\) to warn [Shanna](/wiki/Shanna_the_She-Devil "Shanna the She-Devil") of an unknown mutant threat, [Nekra](/wiki/Nekra "Nekra").[\[29]](#cite_note-29) In *[The Defenders](/wiki/The_Defenders_(comic_book) "The Defenders (comic book)")* \#15–16 (Sept–Oct. 1974\), written by [Len Wein](/wiki/Len_Wein "Len Wein"), Xavier teamed up with the Defenders to battle against Magneto and his reformed Brotherhood of Evil Mutants. It was in this battle that Magneto and the Brotherhood were reduced to infancy by [Alpha, the Ultimate Mutant](/wiki/Alpha_the_Ultimate_Mutant "Alpha the Ultimate Mutant").[\[30]](#cite_note-30) Xavier made one final solo appearance during this period in *Giant\-Size Fantastic Four* \#4 (Feb. 1975\), where he assisted the Fantastic Four in subduing [Jamie Madrox, the Multiple Man](/wiki/Multiple_Man "Multiple Man").[\[31]](#cite_note-31)



### 1975–1991: Chris Claremont era

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: 1975–1991: Chris Claremont era")]


Writer [Chris Claremont](/wiki/Chris_Claremont "Chris Claremont") looks through a copy of a hardcover *X\-Men* volume that collects many of the issues he wrote, March 2012\.


*X\-Men* was relaunched in May 1975 with *[Giant\-Size X\-Men](/wiki/Giant-Size_X-Men "Giant-Size X-Men")* \#1, by [Len Wein](/wiki/Len_Wein "Len Wein") and [Dave Cockrum](/wiki/Dave_Cockrum "Dave Cockrum").[\[32]](#cite_note-32) The title featured a new, international team consisting of Scott Summers (Cyclops) of the United States, [Ireland](/wiki/Ireland "Ireland")'s [Sean Cassidy (Banshee)](/wiki/Banshee_(comics) "Banshee (comics)"), the [Japanese](/wiki/Japan "Japan") mutant [Shiro Yoshida (Sunfire)](/wiki/Sunfire_(comics) "Sunfire (comics)"), and [James "Logan" Howlett (Wolverine)](/wiki/Wolverine_(character) "Wolverine (character)") from [Canada](/wiki/Canada "Canada"), along with new characters [Ororo Munroe (Storm)](/wiki/Storm_(Marvel_Comics) "Storm (Marvel Comics)") out of [Kenya](/wiki/Kenya "Kenya"), the [German](/wiki/Germany "Germany") [Kurt Wagner (Nightcrawler)](/wiki/Nightcrawler_(comics) "Nightcrawler (comics)"), [Piotr "Peter" Rasputin (Colossus)](/wiki/Colossus_(comics) "Colossus (comics)") from [Russia](/wiki/Russia "Russia") in [The Soviet Union](/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"), and [John Proudstar (Thunderbird)](/wiki/Thunderbird_(John_Proudstar) "Thunderbird (John Proudstar)"), a Native American. The original plan was to continue *Giant\-Size X\-Men* as a quarterly, but instead original stories were printed in the book, again initially bimonthly.[\[8]](#cite_note-defalco-8)
[Chris Claremont](/wiki/Chris_Claremont "Chris Claremont")'s first issue as writer, \#94, featured all the original X\-Men leaving the team with the exception of Cyclops. Sunfire also left, having agreed to assist the X\-Men on one successful mission only. Thunderbird was killed in \#95\.[\[33]](#cite_note-Gilbert-p171-33) [Moira MacTaggert](/wiki/Moira_MacTaggert "Moira MacTaggert"), a human ally of the X\-Men, and later to be established as a former fiancé of Xavier, debuted in \#96\.[\[33]](#cite_note-Gilbert-p171-33) Marvel Girl became [Phoenix](/wiki/Phoenix_Force_(comics) "Phoenix Force (comics)") in issue \#101\.[\[34]](#cite_note-34) This was followed by the first [Shi'ar](/wiki/Shi%27ar "Shi'ar") [space opera](/wiki/Space_opera "Space opera") story. Cockrum was replaced as penciller by [John Byrne](/wiki/John_Byrne_(comics) "John Byrne (comics)") as of \#108\.[\[35]](#cite_note-35) Byrne became co\-plotter, and during his run the series became a monthly title again.[\[8]](#cite_note-defalco-8)


The series title was changed to *The Uncanny X\-Men* with issue \#114 (October 1978\).


For the remainder of the decade, the X\-Men fought enemies such as [Stephen Lang](/wiki/Stephen_Lang_(comics) "Stephen Lang (comics)") and his Sentinels, Magneto, Banshee's cousin [Black Tom Cassidy](/wiki/Black_Tom_Cassidy "Black Tom Cassidy") and Cain Marko/the Juggernaut, the Shi'ar [Erik the Red](/wiki/Erik_the_Red_(comics) "Erik the Red (comics)") and the [Imperial Guard](/wiki/Imperial_Guard_(Marvel_Comics) "Imperial Guard (Marvel Comics)"), [Arcade](/wiki/Arcade_(Marvel_Comics) "Arcade (Marvel Comics)"), Wolverine's former colleagues, the Canadian superhero team [Alpha Flight](/wiki/Alpha_Flight "Alpha Flight"),[\[36]](#cite_note-36) and MacTaggert's son [Proteus](/wiki/Proteus_(Marvel_Comics) "Proteus (Marvel Comics)").[\[37]](#cite_note-37) In 2010, [Comics Bulletin](/wiki/Comics_Bulletin "Comics Bulletin") ranked Claremont and Byrne's run on *The X\-Men* second on its list of the "Top 10 1970s Marvels".[\[38]](#cite_note-38)


The "[Dark Phoenix Saga](/wiki/Dark_Phoenix_Saga "Dark Phoenix Saga")" in 1980 led to a change in the line\-up of the team, with the death of Phoenix (Jean Grey),[\[39]](#cite_note-39) and Cyclops leaving the team to mourn her. Comics writers and historians Roy Thomas and [Peter Sanderson](/wiki/Peter_Sanderson "Peter Sanderson") observed that "'The Dark Phoenix Saga' is to Claremont and Byrne what '[the Galactus Trilogy](/wiki/The_Galactus_Trilogy "The Galactus Trilogy")' is to Stan Lee and Jack Kirby. It is a landmark in Marvel history, showcasing its creators' work at the height of their abilities."[\[40]](#cite_note-40)[\[41]](#cite_note-41) The storyline also saw the introduction of recurring antagonists the [Hellfire Club](/wiki/Hellfire_Club_(comics) "Hellfire Club (comics)"), and its Inner Circle consisting of [Sebastian Shaw](/wiki/Sebastian_Shaw_(comics) "Sebastian Shaw (comics)"), [Emma Frost](/wiki/Emma_Frost "Emma Frost"), [Harry Leland](/wiki/Harry_Leland "Harry Leland"), [Donald Pierce](/wiki/Donald_Pierce "Donald Pierce"), along with [Mastermind](/wiki/Mastermind_(Jason_Wyngarde) "Mastermind (Jason Wyngarde)"), previously a member of Magneto's Brotherhood. Teenage mutant [Katherine Anne "Kitty" Pryde/Shadowcat](/wiki/Kitty_Pryde "Kitty Pryde") was introduced in \#129 (Jan. 1980\) and joined the X\-Men in \#139\. [Alison Blaire/the Dazzler](/wiki/Dazzler_(comics) "Dazzler (comics)"), a disco\-singing, roller\-skating mutant, was introduced in \#130 (Feb. 1980\), but did not join the team, instead having a solo title.


A new Brotherhood of Evil Mutants, led by [Mystique](/wiki/Mystique_(comics) "Mystique (comics)"), was introduced in the "[Days of Future Past](/wiki/Days_of_Future_Past "Days of Future Past")" storyline (\#141–142, Jan–Feb 1981\) in which a time\-travelling Katherine Anne "Kitty" Pryde/Shadowcat tried to avert a dystopian future caused by the Brotherhood assassinating Presidential candidate Senator [Robert Kelly](/wiki/Robert_Kelly_(comics) "Robert Kelly (comics)").[\[42]](#cite_note-42) Byrne plotted the story wanting to depict the Sentinels as a genuine threat to the existence of the mutant race.[\[43]](#cite_note-43) He then left the series after \#143, being replaced by a returning Cockrum, who in turn was succeeded by [Paul Smith](/wiki/Paul_Smith_(comics) "Paul Smith (comics)") and [John Romita Jr.](/wiki/John_Romita_Jr. "John Romita Jr.")


By the mid\-1980s, *The Uncanny X\-Men* had become one of the best\-selling [American comic books](/wiki/American_comic_book "American comic book"),[\[44]](#cite_note-44) turning many of the writers and illustrators into industry stars and leading to numerous spin\-offs and [miniseries](/wiki/Miniseries "Miniseries").


Erik Magnus Lehnsherr/Magneto was gradually revealed to be more complex: \#150 established that he was a survivor of the [Holocaust](/wiki/Holocaust "Holocaust"), and in \#161 it is shown that Erik Magnus Lehnsherr/Magneto and Professor Charles Xavier had known each other before Xavier had founded the X\-Men. [Anna Marie LeBeau/Rogue](/wiki/Rogue_(comics) "Rogue (comics)"), a member of [Raven Darkholme/Mystique](/wiki/Mystique_(comics) "Mystique (comics)")'s Brotherhood of Evil Mutants, defected to the X\-Men in \#171 (July 1983\). Raven Darkholme/Mystique's Brotherhood of Evil Mutants changed sides and became the government\-backed [Freedom Force](/wiki/Freedom_Force_(comics) "Freedom Force (comics)") in \#199\. Their first action was to capture Erik Magnus Lehnsherr/Magneto, who had begun associating with the X\-Men during the "[Secret Wars II](/wiki/Secret_Wars_II "Secret Wars II")" crossover. Erik Magnus Lehnsherr/Magneto surrenders himself, but escapes after his trial is abandoned, he takes over the headmastership of the school after Xavier leaves for space in \#200 (Dec. 1985\).


The [Morlocks](/wiki/Morlocks_(comics) "Morlocks (comics)"), a group of disfigured mutants living underneath [New York City](/wiki/New_York,_New_York "New York, New York"), were introduced in \#169 (May 1983\). Storm became their leader in \#170\. She was de\-powered accidentally by government forces aiming for Anna Marie LeBeau/Rogue, and met [Forge](/wiki/Forge_(comics) "Forge (comics)"),[\[45]](#cite_note-45) a mutant with the power of invention. After Storm left the team temporarily to return to her native Africa, Nightcrawler became field leader.


The character [Rachel Summers](/wiki/Rachel_Summers "Rachel Summers") from the future [dystopia](/wiki/Dystopia "Dystopia") presented in "Days of Future Past" had been shown to arrive in the present day in *New Mutants* \#18, and then made appearances in *Uncanny X\-Men* from \#184 on and was revealed to be Cyclops' daughter.


Claremont attempted to write Scott Summers/Cyclops out of the series, by having him marry [Madelyne Pryor](/wiki/Madelyne_Pryor "Madelyne Pryor") in \#175 (Nov. 1983\); she gave birth to his son in \#201 (Jan. 1986\). The *[X\-Factor](/wiki/X-Factor_(comics) "X-Factor (comics)")* series was launched two months later and featured the original five X\-Men. This meant the resurrection of Jean Grey (performed by [retcon](/wiki/Retroactive_continuity "Retroactive continuity"), the character appearing from \#101 having never really been her), and having Scott Summers/Cyclops abandon his wife and child. Claremont strongly objected to the latter, and was hostile towards the title until [Louise Simonson](/wiki/Louise_Simonson "Louise Simonson") became writer.[\[8]](#cite_note-defalco-8)


Artist [Arthur Adams](/wiki/Arthur_Adams_(comics) "Arthur Adams (comics)") began a long association with the team by drawing *The Uncanny X\-Men Annual* \#9 (1985\) and would serve as the artist on several of the *Annuals* in the next few years.[\[46]](#cite_note-46)


The end of 1986 saw the first [crossover](/wiki/Fictional_crossover "Fictional crossover") between X\-Men titles, the "[Mutant Massacre](/wiki/Mutant_Massacre "Mutant Massacre")", which saw a large number of Morlocks killed by the [Marauders](/wiki/Marauders_(comics) "Marauders (comics)"), acting under orders from the mysterious [Nathaniel Essex/Mister Sinister](/wiki/Mister_Sinister "Mister Sinister")[\[47]](#cite_note-47) The late 1980s saw several other crossovers: 1988's "[Fall of the Mutants](/wiki/Fall_of_the_Mutants "Fall of the Mutants")"[\[48]](#cite_note-48) and 1989's "[Inferno](/wiki/Inferno_(Marvel_Comics) "Inferno (Marvel Comics)")", which resolved the issue of Madelyne Pryor by revealing her to have been a clone of Jean Grey created by Sinister. The cast was shaken up, with the addition of [Psylocke](/wiki/Betsy_Braddock "Betsy Braddock"), the Dazzler, [Longshot](/wiki/Longshot_(Marvel_Comics) "Longshot (Marvel Comics)") and Havok in early 1987, as well as the first appearances of NPR\-TV reporter Manoli Wetherell in \#226 (1988\),[\[49]](#cite_note-49) new teenage mutant [Jubilation Lee/Jubilee](/wiki/Jubilee_(comics) "Jubilee (comics)") in \#244 (1989\), and [Remy LeBeau/Gambit](/wiki/Gambit_(Marvel_Comics) "Gambit (Marvel Comics)")[\[50]](#cite_note-50) in *Uncanny X\-Men* \#266 (1990\). The X\-Men left their traditional residence in [Westchester County, New York](/wiki/Westchester_County,_New_York "Westchester County, New York"), and lived variously on [Alcatraz](/wiki/Alcatraz "Alcatraz"), [Muir Island](/wiki/Muir_Island "Muir Island") and in the [Australian outback](/wiki/Australian_outback "Australian outback"). The "[X\-Tinction Agenda](/wiki/X-Tinction_Agenda "X-Tinction Agenda")" crossover, in which the X\-Men, *X\-Factor* and the *New Mutants* fight against the government of [Genosha](/wiki/Genosha "Genosha") for mutant rights, was published in the fall of 1990\.[\[51]](#cite_note-51)


The title became twice\-monthly from 1988 to 1990 every summer, and helped to launch the careers of artists [Marc Silvestri](/wiki/Marc_Silvestri "Marc Silvestri") and [Jim Lee](/wiki/Jim_Lee "Jim Lee"). In 1991 another *X\-Men* title was launched, titled simply *[X\-Men](/wiki/X-Men:_Legacy "X-Men: Legacy")*;[\[52]](#cite_note-52) both titles were now published monthly. Claremont wrote the first three issue of this series, in which the X\-Factor and X\-Men teams reunited with Professor Xavier at the school. Claremont left Marvel after disputes with [Bob Harras](/wiki/Bob_Harras "Bob Harras") and artist [Jim Lee](/wiki/Jim_Lee "Jim Lee") (of *X\-Men*). Claremont's final issue of *Uncanny X\-Men* was \#279, during the "[Muir Island Saga](/wiki/Muir_Island_Saga "Muir Island Saga")", which is set before those events.[\[8]](#cite_note-defalco-8)[\[53]](#cite_note-official_index-53)



### 1991–2011: Post\-Claremont era

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: 1991–2011: Post-Claremont era")]


[Jim Lee](/wiki/Jim_Lee "Jim Lee") holding some of the 1990s issues of the series on which he rose to stardom as an artist


After Claremont's run, the X\-Men were divided into two color\-coded squads, with a Blue team headlining the adjectiveless *X\-Men* title, while the Gold team, consisting of Warren Worthington III/Archangel, Piotr "Peter" Rasputin/Colossus, Jean Grey, Robert "Bobby" Drake/the Iceman and Ororo Munroe/Storm, appeared in *Uncanny*. This roster was later joined by [Lucas Bishop](/wiki/Bishop_(comics) "Bishop (comics)"), another refugee from the future.[\[54]](#cite_note-54) After Claremont's departure, Jim Lee continued as plotter, while John Byrne scripted from \#281–286\. Byrne was replaced as scripter from \#287 by [Scott Lobdell](/wiki/Scott_Lobdell "Scott Lobdell"), who was fully credited as writer from \#289\. The "[X\-Cutioner's Song](/wiki/X-Cutioner%27s_Song "X-Cutioner's Song")" crossover was released in the fall of 1992[\[55]](#cite_note-55) and resulted in the outbreak of the [Legacy virus](/wiki/Legacy_virus "Legacy virus"), a mutant\-specific plague which continued as a story element in X\-Men comics until 2001\.[\[53]](#cite_note-official_index-53)


Crossovers continued through the 1990s. The "[Fatal Attractions](/wiki/Fatal_Attractions_(comics) "Fatal Attractions (comics)")" crossover of 1993 saw the X\-Men battle Magneto again, and the "[Phalanx Covenant](/wiki/Phalanx_Covenant "Phalanx Covenant")" story of 1994 focused mostly on the techno\-organic [Phalanx](/wiki/Phalanx_(comics) "Phalanx (comics)"). *Uncanny X\-Men* briefly ceased publication during the "[Age of Apocalypse](/wiki/Age_of_Apocalypse "Age of Apocalypse")" storyline in 1995, which dealt with an alternative present[\[56]](#cite_note-56) created by a time\-travelling assassin killing Xavier; it was replaced by *[Astonishing X\-Men](/wiki/Astonishing_X-Men "Astonishing X-Men")*. Lobdell was writing *X\-Men* as well from 1995\.


Lobdell was replaced by [Steven T. Seagle](/wiki/Steven_T._Seagle "Steven T. Seagle") with issue \#350 (Dec. 1997\). He was replaced in turn with [Alan Davis](/wiki/Alan_Davis "Alan Davis"), as plotter, from issue \#366 (Mar. 1999\) to \#380\. Davis's run included "[the Twelve](/wiki/Apocalypse:_The_Twelve "Apocalypse: The Twelve")" crossover from \#370–375, in which Apocalypse sought the only 12 mutants, which also ran in his *X\-Men* title, again being treated as a biweekly publication. As part of the [Revolution](/wiki/Revolution_(2000_comic_book) "Revolution (2000 comic book)") relaunch, Chris Claremont made a brief return from \#381 (June 2000\) to \#389, at which point he transferred to the new *[X\-Treme X\-Men](/wiki/X-Treme_X-Men "X-Treme X-Men")* title, as [Grant Morrison](/wiki/Grant_Morrison "Grant Morrison") took over *X\-Men* (vol. 2\)[\[57]](#cite_note-57) and that became the flagship X\-Men title.[\[53]](#cite_note-official_index-53) From 2001, Lobdell made a short return, and then [Joe Casey](/wiki/Joe_Casey "Joe Casey") and [Chuck Austen](/wiki/Chuck_Austen "Chuck Austen") wrote runs into 2004\. The title became bimonthly from 2003 to 2004\.[\[53]](#cite_note-official_index-53)


The *[X\-Men: Reload](/wiki/X-Men:_Reload "X-Men: Reload")* reshuffle of titles in 2004 led to Claremont returning to *Uncanny* with issue \#444\. The stories addressed the new status quo established by Morrison. Claremont remained until \#473\. His final story was "[End of the Greys](/wiki/End_of_Greys "End of Greys")" in 2006, as part of the "[Decimation](/wiki/Decimation_(comics) "Decimation (comics)")" storyline, where the vast majority of mutants had lost their powers. He was replaced by [Ed Brubaker](/wiki/Ed_Brubaker "Ed Brubaker"), who wrote a 12\-part epic space opera story "The Rise and Fall of the Shi'ar Empire", as a follow\-up to his miniseries *[X\-Men: Deadly Genesis](/wiki/X-Men:_Deadly_Genesis "X-Men: Deadly Genesis")*. After this, the title led into the "[Messiah Complex](/wiki/X-Men:_Messiah_Complex "X-Men: Messiah Complex")" crossover event, dealing with the first mutant birth since the Decimation.


[Matt Fraction](/wiki/Matt_Fraction "Matt Fraction") became co\-author from \#500, and sole author from \#504\. The entire X\-Men team relocated to San Francisco – first to the city, and then, after the "[Utopia](/wiki/Utopia_(comics) "Utopia (comics)")" crossover with *[Dark Avengers](/wiki/Dark_Avengers "Dark Avengers")*, to an island named Utopia in [San Francisco Bay](/wiki/San_Francisco_Bay "San Francisco Bay").[\[58]](#cite_note-cbr_uncanny_500-58)[\[59]](#cite_note-59) The *[Nation X](/wiki/X-Men:_Nation_X "X-Men: Nation X")* storyline focused on the return of the re\-powered Magneto, and him coming to Utopia.[\[60]](#cite_note-60) The *[Second Coming](/wiki/Second_Coming_(comics) "Second Coming (comics)")* crossover saw the return of [Hope Summers](/wiki/Hope_Summers_(comics) "Hope Summers (comics)"), the baby from the "Messiah Complex" arc, to the present day, as a young adult; and the emergence of the "Five Lights", the first new mutants to have arisen (apart from Hope) since the Decimation. [Nightcrawler](/wiki/Nightcrawler_(comics) "Nightcrawler (comics)") was killed during this storyline and the [Beast](/wiki/Beast_(comics) "Beast (comics)") left in protest after his discovery of Cyclops' secret death squad [X\-Force](/wiki/X-Force_(comics) "X-Force (comics)").[\[61]](#cite_note-61)[\[62]](#cite_note-62)


[Kieron Gillen](/wiki/Kieron_Gillen "Kieron Gillen") took over co\-authorship of the series with \#531, and became sole writer from \#534\.1\.[\[63]](#cite_note-63)[\[64]](#cite_note-64)



### 2012: Volume 2

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: 2012: Volume 2")]
The original series ended with \#544 and relaunched as a new volume after the events of the *[X\-Men: Schism](/wiki/X-Men:_Schism "X-Men: Schism")* miniseries, wherein half the X\-Men, led by Wolverine, returned to New York, to found a new school.[\[65]](#cite_note-65)[\[66]](#cite_note-66) The new volume featured the Extinction Team, containing members of the X\-Men whom Cyclops had retained to deal with potential threats to the mutant race's survival. Gillen's run led into, and crossed over with, the *[Avengers vs. X\-Men](/wiki/Avengers_vs._X-Men "Avengers vs. X-Men")* storyline and finished with issue \#20 in October 2012\. The volume ended with Cyclops, who had become increasingly hardline during Gillen's run, in prison for his actions during that storyline.[\[67]](#cite_note-67) Gillen wrote a five\-part epilogue, *AvX: Consequences*.[\[68]](#cite_note-68)



### 2013–2015: Volume 3

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: 2013–2015: Volume 3")]
As part of [Marvel NOW!](/wiki/Marvel_NOW! "Marvel NOW!"), a new volume of *Uncanny X\-Men* was launched in February 2013 with an April 2013 cover date,[\[69]](#cite_note-69) written by Brian Michael Bendis, who is also writing another X\-Men title, *[All\-New X\-Men](/wiki/All-New_X-Men "All-New X-Men")*, and drawn by Chris Bachalo.[\[70]](#cite_note-AllNewXMen-70) It features [Cyclops](/wiki/Cyclops_(Marvel_Comics) "Cyclops (Marvel Comics)") and remnants of his Extinction Team recruiting new mutants to help them prepare for what Cyclops believes to be an inevitable revolution, coinciding events of the first *All\-New X\-Men* story arc.[\[71]](#cite_note-vol3-71) This volume saw Cyclops leading his team to an abandoned [Weapon X](/wiki/Weapon_X "Weapon X") facility to train new recruits and prepare for impending war against the humans, who see Cyclops as a terrorist's due to his actions in *Avengers Vs. X\-Men*. Eventually, Kitty Pryde and the time\-displaced X\-Men join his cause after facing a team of X\-Men from a dystopian future.[\[72]](#cite_note-72) It lasted 36 issues, with the final issue reverting to the legacy numbering of *Uncanny X\-Men* \#600\.



### 2016–2017: Volume 4

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: 2016–2017: Volume 4")]
As part of [All\-New, All\-Different Marvel](/wiki/All-New,_All-Different_Marvel "All-New, All-Different Marvel"), *Uncanny X\-Men* was relaunched, written by [Cullen Bunn](/wiki/Cullen_Bunn "Cullen Bunn") with art by [Greg Land](/wiki/Greg_Land "Greg Land"). The relaunched *Uncanny X\-Men* team features Magneto leading Psylocke, the Archangel, M, Mystique, [Fantomex](/wiki/Fantomex "Fantomex") and Sabretooth, while a different team led by Storm will be called the [Extraordinary X\-Men](/wiki/Extraordinary_X-Men "Extraordinary X-Men"). Cyclops's fate after Battleworld is shown to us in the *Death of X* miniseries (Cyclops was exposed to the Terrigen Mist and died from M\-Pox).[\[73]](#cite_note-SDCCPanel-73)


The tagline for the relaunched series is "Bigger threats require more threatening X\-Men", and is considered to be a continuation of Bunn's previous work on the *Magneto* solo series. The series will deal with threats that arise as a result of a new, more dangerous world post\-*[Secret Wars](/wiki/Secret_Wars_(2015_comic_book) "Secret Wars (2015 comic book)")*. Summing up the team, Bunn states "They're upholding Xavier's dream, but they have no right to do so."[\[73]](#cite_note-SDCCPanel-73)



### 2019: Volume 5

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: 2019: Volume 5")]
Announced in August 2018, *Uncanny X\-Men* (vol. 5\) debuted November 14, 2018, with the weekly 10\-part "X\-Men: Disassembled" arc and follows on from the events of the *[Extermination](/wiki/Extermination_(comics) "Extermination (comics)")* miniseries.[\[74]](#cite_note-74) This incarnation of the team features [Lucas Bishop](/wiki/Bishop_(comics) "Bishop (comics)"), [Kurt Wagner/Nightcrawler](/wiki/Nightcrawler_(comics) "Nightcrawler (comics)"), [Jean Grey](/wiki/Jean_Grey "Jean Grey"), [Ororo Munroe/Storm](/wiki/Storm_(comics) "Storm (comics)"), [Elizabeth "Betsy" Braddock/Psylocke](/wiki/Betsy_Braddock "Betsy Braddock"), [Jean\-Paul Beaubier/Northstar](/wiki/Northstar_(character) "Northstar (character)"), [Robert "Bobby" Drake/the Iceman](/wiki/Iceman_(Marvel_Comics) "Iceman (Marvel Comics)"), [Hank McCoy/the Beast](/wiki/Beast_(comics) "Beast (comics)"), [Laura Kinney/X\-23](/wiki/X-23 "X-23"), [Lorna Dane/Polaris](/wiki/Polaris_(Marvel_Comics) "Polaris (Marvel Comics)"), [Jubilation Lee/Jubilee](/wiki/Jubilee_(comics) "Jubilee (comics)"), [Katherine Anne "Kitty" Pryde/Shadowcat](/wiki/Kitty_Pryde "Kitty Pryde") and [Sam Guthrie/Cannonball](/wiki/Cannonball_(comics) "Cannonball (comics)") as well as trainee X\-Men [Hisako Ichiki/Armor](/wiki/Armor_(comics) "Armor (comics)"), [Victor Borkowski/Anole](/wiki/Anole_(comics) "Anole (comics)"), [Megan Gwynn/Pixie](/wiki/Pixie_(X-Men) "Pixie (X-Men)"), [Idie Okonkwo/Oya](/wiki/Oya_(comics) "Oya (comics)"), [Robert Herman/the Glob](/wiki/Glob_Herman "Glob Herman"), [Santo Vaccarro/Rockslide](/wiki/Rockslide_(comics) "Rockslide (comics)"). Following the 10th issue, the series began focusing on a new team of X\-Men featuring [Scott Summers/Cyclops](/wiki/Cyclops_(Marvel_Comics) "Cyclops (Marvel Comics)"), [James "Logan" Howlett/Wolverine](/wiki/Wolverine_(character) "Wolverine (character)"), [Alex Summers/Havok](/wiki/Havok_(comics) "Havok (comics)"), [Jamie Madrox/the Multiple Man](/wiki/Multiple_Man "Multiple Man"), [Rahne Sinclair/Wolfsbane](/wiki/Wolfsbane_(comics) "Wolfsbane (comics)"), [Illyana Rasputin/Magik](/wiki/Magik_(Illyana_Rasputina) "Magik (Illyana Rasputina)"), [Danielle Moonstar/Mirage](/wiki/Dani_Moonstar "Dani Moonstar"), [Xi'an Coy Minh/Karma](/wiki/Karma_(comics) "Karma (comics)").


After this volume, all X\-Men titles were cancelled and two intertwining six\-issue miniseries written by [Jonathan Hickman](/wiki/Jonathan_Hickman "Jonathan Hickman"), called "[House of X](/wiki/House_of_X_and_Powers_of_X "House of X and Powers of X")" and "[Powers of X](/wiki/Powers_of_X "Powers of X")",[\[75]](#cite_note-75) began a weekly run in July 2019 and concluded on October of the same year. Shortly after those were completed, the [X\-Men series relaunched](/wiki/X-Men_(comic_book)#Volume_5_(2020_series) "X-Men (comic book)") with *X\-Men* \#1, accompanied by the other related teams' regular series, such as *[Marauders](/wiki/Marauders_(comics) "Marauders (comics)")*, *[X\-Force](/wiki/X-Force "X-Force")*, *[Excalibur](/wiki/Excalibur_(comics) "Excalibur (comics)")*, *[New Mutants](/wiki/New_Mutants "New Mutants")*, *X\-Corp*, *[Wolverine](/wiki/Wolverine_(character) "Wolverine (character)")*, *[X\-Men: Giant Size](/wiki/Giant-Size_X-Men "Giant-Size X-Men")*, and reviving 1987's *[Fallen Angels](/wiki/Fallen_Angels_(comics) "Fallen Angels (comics)")*; all part of the 2019 story arc "[Dawn of X](/wiki/Dawn_of_X "Dawn of X")", which searches to unite all mutantdom and settle down as a whole species.



### 2024\-present: Volume 6

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: 2024-present: Volume 6")]
It was announced that a new *Uncanny X\-Men* series would return. The first issue is scheduled to be released on August 7, 2024, cover\-dated October 2024, and will be written by [Gail Simone](/wiki/Gail_Simone "Gail Simone") and penciled by [David Marquez](/wiki/David_Marquez_(comics) "David Marquez (comics)"). The members of this team will be: Wolverine, Gambit, Jubilee, Rogue, and Nightcrawler.

### *Annuals*

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: Annuals")]
Like many comic book series, *Uncanny X\-Men* had an associated double\-sized [*Annual*](/wiki/Annual_publication "Annual publication") series, once in both 1970 and 1971, then regularly from 1979 to 2001\.[\[76]](#cite_note-76)[\[77]](#cite_note-77) A second series of *Uncanny X\-Men Annuals* began in 2006 as volume 2 issue \#1\.[\[78]](#cite_note-78)

In *Amazing Adventures* \#11 (March 1972\), it was revealed in a flashback that Hank McCoy/Beast had left the group sometime before and accepted a position with the Brand Corporation. In another flashback recounted in *Incredible Hulk* \#150 (April 1972\), Alex Summers/Havok and Lorna Dane/Polaris also left the group soon after, due to a dispute between Havok and Iceman over Polaris, and moved to New Mexico. ### Timeline

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Timeline")]



Notes

This is an article about the comic book, and thus the publication history, not the in\-continuity history. As such, the above reflects the team roster for the book at time of publication. Similarly, this article only reflects the team roster for the X\-Men team whose home is this publication.


Professor X is the Headmaster of Xavier's School for Gifted Youngsters and mentor to the X\-Men, but he is rarely (if ever) a member of the X\-Men team. In his role as mentor, he has typically been present in the book, but he has notable absences, including issues \#43–64 (dead, later retconned as preparing for the Z'Nox), \#200–273 (with [Lilandra Neramani](/wiki/Lilandra_Neramani "Lilandra Neramani") in Shi'ar space; replaced as Headmaster by Magneto during most of this absence), \#340–351 (in government custody after the Onslaught crisis), \#379–386 (educating [Cadre K](/wiki/Cadre_K "Cadre K") in space), and \#495–513 (rebuilding his mind in *X\-Men: Legacy*).


Jean Grey was replaced by the Phoenix Force from issues \#101–137\. This was a [retcon](/wiki/Retroactive_continuity "Retroactive continuity") that was only revealed years later.


At many times, the team roster has been the same as that appearing in *X\-Men* (vol. 2\) and during two periods, the two books have even been treated by their writer as a single bi\-weekly title (issues \#289–350 by Scott Lobdell and issues \#366–380 by Alan Davis).


During issues \#370–372, Wolverine was replaced by a Skrull infiltrator, leading to "The Shattering"/"The Twelve" storylines, and the *Astonishing X\-Men* (vol. 2\) limited series.


After moving to San Francisco, many other mutants continually appear as background characters or allies, but apart from during crossovers they are rarely considered part of the team roster.


After the series was relaunched as *Uncanny X\-Men* \#1, it featured Cyclops's "Extinction Team", composed of himself, Emma Frost, Namor, Magneto, Storm, Hope Summers, Colossus, Danger and Magik; Psylocke was briefly a member of this team while Emma was injured.



Title
-----

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Title")]


| Issues | Title | Indicia title |
| --- | --- | --- |
| \#1–49 | *The X\-Men* | *The X\-Men* |
| \#50–93 | *X\-Men* |
| \#94–113 | *X\-Men* |
| \#114–138 | *The Uncanny X\-Men* |
| \#139–141 | *The X\-Men* |
| \#142–393 | *The Uncanny X\-Men* |
| \#394–407 | *Uncanny X\-Men* |
| \#408–544, \#600;Vol. 2 \#1–20;Vol. 3 \#1–35;Vol. 4 \#1–19;Vol. 5 \#1–22 | *Uncanny X\-Men* |


Until 2011, *Uncanny X\-Men* remained Marvel Comics' only [Silver Age](/wiki/Silver_Age_of_Comic_Books#Marvel_Comics "Silver Age of Comic Books") title to retain its consecutive issue numbering since its conception, even during the early 1970s reprint hiatus. *[The Amazing Spider\-Man](/wiki/The_Amazing_Spider-Man "The Amazing Spider-Man")*, *[The Avengers](/wiki/Avengers_(comics) "Avengers (comics)")*, *[The Fantastic Four](/wiki/The_Fantastic_Four "The Fantastic Four")* and other legacy titles have all, at one time or another, restarted their numbering at \#1, though all later returned to their original numbering. The final issue to be published under the original numbering was \#544, published in October 2011 with a December 2011 [cover date](/wiki/Cover_date "Cover date"), which was followed by a new \#1 the following month. In 2015, Marvel released *Uncanny X\-Men* \#600, following Vol. 3 \#35, as a conclusion to the Brian Michael Bendis' run on both *[All New X\-Men](/wiki/All_New_X-Men "All New X-Men")* and Vol. 3\.


From issue \#1\-93 the [indicia](/wiki/Indicia_(publishing) "Indicia (publishing)") title was ***The X\-Men***. After the relaunch with issue \#94, and up to \#138, the article *The* was dropped from the indicia title, making it ***X\-Men***, but the article was added back in issues \#139–141\.


The title ***The Uncanny X\-Men*** was first used in the issue \#95 title block following the "Stan Lee Presents:" tagline, though the title did not appear on the covers or indicia titles yet; covers begin displaying this title in \#114\. Beginning with issue \#142[\[79]](#cite_note-79) and up to \#407, the indicia title was finally changed to *The Uncanny X\-Men*. Issue \#408 was the first to use the indicia title ***Uncanny X\-Men***.


A separate series, titled simply *[X\-Men](/wiki/X-Men:_Legacy "X-Men: Legacy")*, launched with an October 1991 [cover date](/wiki/Cover_date "Cover date").

In other media
--------------

\[[edit](/w/index.php?title=Uncanny_X-Men&action=edit&section=58 "Edit section: In other media")]
* The title card of *[The Super Hero Squad Show](/wiki/The_Super_Hero_Squad_Show "The Super Hero Squad Show")* episode "Hexed, Vexed and Perplexed!" is an homage to *X\-Men* \#1\.\[*[citation needed](/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")*]
* During a montage in the 2024 [Marvel Cinematic Universe](/wiki/Marvel_Cinematic_Universe "Marvel Cinematic Universe") film *[Deadpool \& Wolverine](/wiki/Deadpool_%26_Wolverine "Deadpool & Wolverine")*, [Wade Wilson](/wiki/Wade_Wilson_(film_character) "Wade Wilson (film character)") encounters a variant of [Wolverine](/wiki/Logan_(film_character) "Logan (film character)") nailed to an X, a reference to the cover art of issue \#251\.[\[80]](#cite_note-80)
